Convergence Analysis of Controlled Particle Systems Arising in Deep Learning: From Finite to Infinite Sample Size
Authors: Huafu Liao, Alpár R. Mészáros, Chenchen Mou, Chao Zhou
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
This paper deals with a class of neural SDEs and studies the limiting behavior of the associated sampled optimal control problems as the sample size grows to infinity. The neural SDEs with N samples can be linked to the N-particle systems with centralized control. We analyze the Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman equation corresponding to the N-particle system and establish regularity results which are uniform in N. The uniform regularity estimates are obtained by the stochastic maximum principle and the analysis of a backward stochastic Riccati equation. Using these uniform regularity results, we show the convergence of the minima of the objective functionals and optimal parameters of the neural SDEs as the sample size N tends to infinity. The limiting objects can be identified with suitable functions defined on the Wasserstein space of Borel probability measures. Furthermore, quantitative convergence rates are also obtained.

Introduction

In recent years, neural networks have been shown very effective for modeling complicated data sets. For situations where large amounts of samples are observed, it is important to ensure the convergence of training outcomes as the number of samples goes to infinity, i.e., the generality of the neural network. Such problems are studied in [20, 54, 65]. Motivated by these studies, in this work we investigate a class of neural SDEs (see [51]), which generalize neural ODEs (see e.g. [17, 35, 63]). As the volume of samples goes to infinity, we establish quantitative results on the convergence of both the minima of the objective functionals and the optimal parameters.

The standing assumptions on the data and the set up for the specific sampled optimal control problems, including the description of the objective function is given in Sect. 2 (see (5) and (7)).

The neural SDE (1) describes deep learning from a dynamical systems viewpoint. The dynamical system approach to deep learning was proposed in [35, 63], and studied later in [7, 24, 25, 40, 61, 65], etc. See also, for instance, [4, 14, 17, 57], for the application of such an approach. Following [35, 48, 63, 65], we explain the intuition behind this approach to deep learning as follows. For models such as residual networks, recurrent neural networks and normalizing flows, etc., given N inputs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x=(x_1,\ldots ,x_N)$$\end{document}x=(x1,…,xN), the typical feed-forward propagation with K layers can be presented as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} x_i(t+1)=x_i(t)+\epsilon f\big (x_i(t),\theta (t)\big ),&\quad x_i(0)=x_i,\quad t=0,1,\ldots ,K-1,\\&\quad i=1,2,\ldots ,N. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}xi(t+1)=xi(t)+ϵf(xi(t),θ(t)),xi(0)=xi,t=0,1,…,K-1,i=1,2,…,N.here x(0), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x(K)\in \mathbb {R}^{dN}$$\end{document}x(K)∈RdN represent the input layer and output layer, respectively, and θ (t)θ(t) is the control (training) parameters. The goal of such learning process is to tune the trainable parameters θ (t)θ(t), t=0,1,… ,K-1t=0,1,…,K-1, so that the terminal state x(K) minimizes/maximizes a given objective function. For example, in supervised learning, the objective function usually requires that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g\big (x_i(K)\big )$$\end{document}g(xi(K)) most resemble certain label \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y_i$$\end{document}yi for i=1,… ,Ni=1,…,N, see [48]; in the context of neural networks for optimal control, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\big (x,\theta (t)\big )$$\end{document}f(x,θ(t)) is understood as a feedback function with θ (t)θ(t) to be determined, the aim is to maximize the expectation of the utility \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{1}{N}\sum _{i=1}^NU\big (x_i(K)\big )$$\end{document}1N∑i=1NU(xi(K)), see [37, 38]. As the layer number K tends to infinity, after an appropriate rescaling ε K=TϵK=T, it is straightforward to present the above iteration via an ODE\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \dot{x}_i(t)=f\big (x_i(t),\theta (t)\big ),\quad x_i(0)=x_i,\quad t\in [0,T],\ i=1,2,\ldots ,N, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}x˙i(t)=f(xi(t),θ(t)),xi(0)=xi,t∈[0,T],i=1,2,…,N,where the terminal state is x(T). Here in a more general setting, we incorporate the feed-forward propagation with a systemic noise as well as the empirical distribution of inputs. The resulting continuous idealization is then naturally generalized to the neural SDE (1). A typical situation where the empirical distribution enters the feed-forward propagation is batch normalization (see [39]). For example, we may take the dynamics in (1) to be\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f(t,\theta ,x,\mu )=\tilde{f}\left( \theta ,\frac{x-\int y\mu (dy)}{\sqrt{\int y^2\mu (dy)+\epsilon }}\right) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f(t,θ,x,μ)=f~θ,x-∫yμ(dy)∫y2μ(dy)+ϵfor some function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{f}$$\end{document}f~, where ε >0ϵ>0 is a given parameter. We note here that the controlled particle system (1) is different from the usual mean field type, typically studied in the literature, in the sense that every particle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^{\theta ,i}_N(t)$$\end{document}XNθ,i(t) in the system shares the same control θ (t)θ(t) rather than having their own \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^i(t)$$\end{document}θi(t).

In summary, our results make it possible to quantitatively analyze the convergence of training outcomes obtained from neural SDEs with N samples when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N\rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}N→+∞. Speaking with the terms of optimal control theory, our problem relates to the convergence of the value functions and optimal controls of the particle systems with centralized controls, i.e., the propagation of chaos or the law of large numbers. As the number of particles grows to infinity, we explore sufficient conditions that ensure the aforementioned convergence. Such convergences are possible thanks to the presence of an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2-regularizer in the objective functional. Furthermore, quantitative results on the convergence rate are also obtained. Above all, our exploration can be interpreted as conditions ensuring the generalization of models in deep learning. Specifically, an adaptation of our results to the analysis on supervised learning is carried out in Sect. 5.

Propagation of chaos on controlled particle systems have been extensively studied recently. To name a few, we refer to [10, 11, 15, 27, 28, 31, 32, 45, 47, 53, 59, 60], see also [16, 18, 19, 23, 34], as well as the references therein for the ones with uncontrolled particle systems. The limit of the value functions in the aforementioned convergence is a function whose state variable is a probability measure. For literature on such limits, see for instance [31, 32, 53, 56, 67].

As mentioned before, our model (1) is significantly different from the ones above in terms of the form of control, which thus results in a very different Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman (HJB) equation. Although similar models are studied in [36–38, 43, 62, 64], their emphasis is on the analysis of the corresponding algorithm. The models and results in [7, 8, 65] are the closest ones to the present paper, where the convergence of both value functions and optimal controls are investigated. In [7, 8] the law of large numbers is obtained where there is no quantitative results. In [65] on the other hand, the authors focus on the deterministic control and obtain quantitative results on large deviations, but the state dynamics f therein is required to be independent of the distribution of particles. Here, we study models with more general state dynamics f that could depend on the distribution of particles and obtain quantitative results. More specifically, besides the law of large numbers, we further show the corresponding convergence rate: as the sample size N grows to infinity, the minima of the objective functional, i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_N$$\end{document}VN and the optimal feedback function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^*_N$$\end{document}θN∗ converge, at certain rates, to a value function and a feedback function whose state variable is the empirical measure of the samples. As a result, we show that the optimal parameters also converge at certain rates. We obtain two kinds of convergence results: the short time convergence and the global convergence, both accompanied by a precise convergence rates.

The HJB equation written for the value function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{N}$$\end{document}VN associated to our main control problem, i.e. Problem 2.2, formally reads as2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\{ \begin{aligned}&\partial _t V_{N}+\frac{\sigma ^2}{2}\sum _{i,j=1}^N\partial ^2_{x_ix_j}V_N+\inf _{\theta \in \Theta }\bigg \{\frac{\lambda }{2}\big |\theta \big |^2+\sum _{i=1}^Nf\bigg (t,\theta ,x_i,\frac{1}{N}\sum _{j=1}^N\delta _{x_j}\bigg )\partial _{x_i}V_N\\&+\frac{1}{N}\sum _{i=1}^NL(x_i)\bigg \}=0,\\&\quad (t,x)\in [0,T)\times \mathbb {R}^N,\\&V_N(T,x_1,\ldots ,x_N)=\frac{1}{N}\sum _{i=1}^NU(x_i),\quad x\in \mathbb {R}^N, \end{aligned}\right. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tVN+σ22∑i,j=1N∂xixj2VN+infθ∈Θ{λ2|θ|2+∑i=1Nf(t,θ,xi,1N∑j=1Nδxj)∂xiVN+1N∑i=1NL(xi)}=0,(t,x)∈[0,T)×RN,VN(T,x1,…,xN)=1N∑i=1NU(xi),x∈RN,where L and U are the suitably chosen loss function and final cost function, respectively, and the control functions are valued in some control set Θ Θ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma \in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}σ∈R and λ >0λ>0 are given further parameters.

To study the convergence of the value functions and optimal feedback functions of Problem 2.2, the main ingredients are the regularity results on the HJB equations which are uniform and decay suitably with respect to N—the dimension of the input variables (Theorems 3.17 and 3.18). This idea is in the spirit of [31] where the control is of the mean field type. However, in contrast to this, in our problem we are faced with the same common control for each particle and the dynamics of each particle could be nonlinear with respect to the control variable. Hence the resulting Hamiltonian is significantly different in structure. Such Hamiltonian requires additional analysis where the a priori estimates on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla _x V_N$$\end{document}∇xVN and the regularizer play important roles. Moreover, in our problem there is a common Brownian motion in the dynamics of each particle. Therefore the method in [31] is no longer applicable directly in our situation. Instead, here we rely more on a probabilistic approach to analyze the HJB equation and to obtain the desired regularity results.

The first main contribution of this paper is the uniform (in N) estimates on the degenerate PDE systems describing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_N$$\end{document}VN, as well as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla _x V_N$$\end{document}∇xVN, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla ^2_{xx}V_N$$\end{document}∇xx2VN. Such uniform estimates will imply the convergence rate of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_N(t,x)$$\end{document}VN(t,x) and the corresponding feedback functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^*_N(t,x)$$\end{document}θN∗(t,x). In order to obtain the desired uniform estimates, we apply the nonlinear Feymann–Kac representation and focus on the stochastic processes corresponding to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_N$$\end{document}VN, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla _x V_N$$\end{document}∇xVN and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla ^2_{xx}V_N$$\end{document}∇xx2VN, respectively. Because of the degenerate nature of the problem, we need to introduce regularizations at several levels: these will be via involving non-degenerate idiosyncratic noise as well as some suitable cut-off procedures to handle the growth properties of the data. Our estimates will turn out to be independent of these regularization parameters. It is well known that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla _x V_N$$\end{document}∇xVN corresponds to the adjoint process in the stochastic maximum principle. As a result of this, we apply the stochastic maximum principle and obtain that each entry of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla _x V_N$$\end{document}∇xVN decays at the rate of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(N^{-1})$$\end{document}O(N-1). However, the analysis of the systems involving \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla ^2_{xx}V_N$$\end{document}∇xx2VN is more subtle. It turns out that the suitable approximations of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla ^2_{xx}V_N$$\end{document}∇xx2VN introduced above, are related to matrix-valued processes (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y_t$$\end{document}Yt in (56) and (70)) that satisfy backward stochastic Riccati equations. We first conduct an analysis on the Hamitonian based on the a priori estimates on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla _xV_N$$\end{document}∇xVN. Then local in time estimates on the processes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y_t$$\end{document}Yt are obtained via the contraction mapping principle: the (i,j)-(i,j)-entry of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y_t$$\end{document}Yt has a decay rate of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\delta _{ij}N^{-1}+N^{-2})$$\end{document}O(δijN-1+N-2). As for the global estimates, we make further suitable convexity assumptions on the data and analyze the eigenvalues of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y_t$$\end{document}Yt utilizing the Riccati (i.e. quadratic) feature of the corresponding BSDE. Under these extra assumptions, each eigenvalue of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y_t$$\end{document}Yt decays at the rate of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(N^{-1})$$\end{document}O(N-1) for arbitrary long time horizon T. These convexity assumptions are similar in spirit to displacement convexity (used in [5, 12, 31]), however, they are not covered by the existing literature (not even by the displacement monotonicity conditions introduced in [3, 33]), because the state dynamics given by f is allowed to have a measure dependence. We note here that such measure dependence of f has been investigated in [6, 28, 45, 55] within the framework of standard mean field games and control.

Our second main contribution is the convergence analysis on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_N(t,x)$$\end{document}VN(t,x) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _N(t,x)$$\end{document}θN(t,x) on a quantitative level. We use a variational approach to show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_N$$\end{document}VN and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^*_N$$\end{document}θN∗ are both finite dimensional projection of certain functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}$$\end{document}V and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^*$$\end{document}θ∗ whose state variables are probability measures. Furthermore, thanks to the previous uniform estimates, we show that, both \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}$$\end{document}V and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^*$$\end{document}θ∗ are Lipschitz continuous with respect to their state variables. Under our two sets of different assumptions, the previous results hold for a short time horizon or global in time, respectively. Such convergence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_N(t,x)$$\end{document}VN(t,x) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _N(t,x)$$\end{document}θN(t,x) has two major implications on neural SDEs. First, the convergence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_N(t,x)$$\end{document}VN(t,x) translates to the convergence of minima of objective functionals. Second, the convergence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _N(t,x)$$\end{document}θN(t,x) would yield pathwise convergence results that translate to the convergence of optimal parameters obtained via neural SDEs (see Propositions 4.5 and 2.7).

Some concluding remarks. The limit function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}$$\end{document}V is formally associated to a second order HJB equation set on the Wasserstein space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}_{2}(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}P2(R). This formally reads as3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\{ \begin{aligned}&\partial _t\mathcal {V}(t,\mu )+\frac{\sigma ^2}{2}\left\{ \int _{\mathbb {R}}\partial _{y\mu }\mathcal {V}(t,\mu )(y)\mu (dy)+\int _{\mathbb {R}^{2}}\partial _{\mu \mu }\mathcal {V}(t,\mu )(y,y')\mu (dy)\mu (dy')\right\} \\&\qquad \ +\inf _{\theta \in \Theta }\left\{ \frac{\lambda }{2}\big |\theta \big |^2+\int _{\mathbb {R}}f\left( t,\theta ,y,\mu \right) \partial _{\mu }\mathcal {V}(t,\mu )(y)\mu (dy)+\int _{\mathbb {R}}L(y)\mu (dy)\right\} =0,\ \ \\&\qquad \qquad \qquad (t,\mu )\in [0,T)\times \mathcal {P}_{2}(\mathbb {R}),\\&\mathcal {V}(T,\mu )=\int _{\mathbb {R}}U(y)\mu (dy),\quad \mu \in \mathcal {P}_{2}(\mathbb {R}). \end{aligned}\right. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tV(t,μ)+σ22∫R∂yμV(t,μ)(y)μ(dy)+∫R2∂μμV(t,μ)(y,y′)μ(dy)μ(dy′)+infθ∈Θλ2|θ|2+∫Rft,θ,y,μ∂μV(t,μ)(y)μ(dy)+∫RL(y)μ(dy)=0,(t,μ)∈[0,T)×P2(R),V(T,μ)=∫RU(y)μ(dy),μ∈P2(R).We would like to underline at this stage that studying the quantitative decay estimates with respect to N of second order spacial derivatives of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{N}$$\end{document}VN (that we perform in this paper) results in the fact that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _{\mu }\mathcal {V}$$\end{document}∂μV exists and it is Lipschitz continuous in a suitable sense. The very same analysis that we perform on these objects could be pushed further, to study quantitative third order derivative estimates for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{N}$$\end{document}VN, which would result in twice differentiability of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}$$\end{document}V, and hence in the fact that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}$$\end{document}V is a classical solution to the HJB Eq. (3). This would be very much in the flavor of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{2,1,w}(\mathcal {P}_{2}(\mathbb {R}))$$\end{document}C2,1,w(P2(R)) type estimates from [31]. When σ =0σ=0, as a consequence of our results, we actually establish the well-posedness of (3) with solutions admitting derivatives that induce Lipschitz continuous maps in the Wasserstein space. We refer to the discussion after Corollary 4.7 for more details. Roughly speaking, the solution in this case is regular enough to let every term in (3) to be meaningful in the classical sense, complementing the results on viscosity solutions in [65].

The specific choice for L, U and f in the above setting is motivated by the concrete applications in deep neural networks we have described above. In our analysis, in fact one would be able to allow more general measure dependent functions in (3).

We would like to emphasize once more that connections between equations of type (2) and (3), and the corresponding quantitative rates of convergence as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N\rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}N→+∞ have received a great attention in the past couple of years. For a non-exhaustive list of works we refer to [9, 11, 27, 32, 53, 58]. The results of these papers differ significantly from ours and their motivation is quite different. Let us spend some time describing the main differences and similarities between our approach and the ones previously considered in the literature. The common feature of the previous works is to start with control problems and HJB equations in the infinite dimensional setting of probability measures, and then project the viscosity solutions onto finite dimensional subspaces (typically the subspace of empirical measures), then study the discrepancy between such projections and the value functions associated to the finite dimensional control/particle systems. The corresponding convergence analysis then can be divided into two distinct categories, essentially based on the notion of viscosity solutions used in the infinite dimensional setting.

First, in [9, 11, 27]—and in related subsequent works—the authors obtain quantitative convergence rates based on Lipschitz and semi-concavity estimates on the infinite dimensional value functions. The rates are proved to be actually sharper in regions where the infinite dimensional value functions are more regular. Classical solutions to the infinite dimensional PDEs, with suitable derivative bounds are known to give rise to sharp convergence rates (cf. [10]). For these methods it was indispensable though the presence of non-degenerate idiosyncratic noise and parabolic regularity.

Second, the line of works [32, 53, 58] considered the presence of purely common noise or deterministic problems, and they have relied on the ‘Hilbertian lifting’ of the infinite dimensional HJB equations. This approach, however, gave only qualitative convergence results in general.

In our models, we consider purely common noise coming from centralized control problems (meaning that all particles share the same control process). Centralized controls do not enter the framework of the previously mentioned works. While these models are relevant in learning applications, to the best our knowledge only [65] has considered such kind of convergence questions in the case of deterministic problems. Furthermore, the above mentioned papers relied on viscosity solution techniques and regularization procedures for semi-concave and Lipschitz continuous functions defined on the Wasserstein space. Our approach is completely different from such methods, and our analysis is based on finite dimensional quantitative analysis and a careful combination of parabolic PDE techniques and stochastic analysis of FBSDE systems. As such, it relies on obtaining uniform in N derivative decay estimates, and obtains the limiting functions via a compactness arguments. Such a method nowadays is referred to as a bottom-up approach.

After the original posting of our manuscript, methods of bottom-up nature became instrumental in tackling other important problems of similar nature: we refer to [21, 22] for the quantitative convergence of Nash equilibria in mean field games and non-exchangeable models and to the works [41, 42, 46, 50] for building solutions to master equations and quantitative convergence problems in the case of so-called mean field games of controls. As this has been done in [42] for a different model, for the last building block in establishing that the limit value function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}$$\end{document}V is a classical solution to (3) in the case when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma \ne 0$$\end{document}σ≠0, we would need the establishment of uniform decay estimates on third order derivatives of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_N$$\end{document}VN. While this should be possible, we believe that such a tedious analysis would significantly deviate attention from the original motivation of our paper, which is the convergence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(V_N)_{N\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(VN)N∈N and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\theta _N)_{N\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}(θN)N∈N. Therefore, we have elected to not include these further arguments in the current manuscript. It worth mentioning again that in the case when σ = 0σ=0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}$$\end{document}V is indeed a classical solution to (3).

In our paper, in addition to the value functions, the convergence rate of control (training) parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _N$$\end{document}θN constitutes a large part of our analysis, whereas most of the above mentioned works focus on the convergence rate of value functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_N$$\end{document}VN only.

The remainder of the paper is organized as follows. In Sect. 2 we describe the model, the main problem of interest and our main results. In Sect. 3 we first introduce the auxiliary problems and study the regularity of the corresponding value functions. Then we establish the estimate on the derivatives of the value function as well as the verification results associated to the original problem. In Sect. 4 we show that the value function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_N$$\end{document}VN in Problem 2.2 is the finite dimensional projection of a function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}$$\end{document}V whose state variable is in the space of probability measures, and establish the results on the convergence rate. In Sect. 5 we adapt our results to the analysis on the training with monotone loss functions.

It is straightforward but notationally cumbersome to generalize our results to the case where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta =\mathbb {R}^d$$\end{document}Θ=Rd and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_i\in \mathbb {R}^m$$\end{document}xi∈Rm. In our analysis we consider the space of Borel probability measures, supported in Euclidean spaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^m$$\end{document}Rm. We work on the specific subset of these measures, which have finite second moment, and denote this by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}_2(\mathbb {R}^m)$$\end{document}P2(Rm). We will always suppose Assumption 2.1 throughout the whole paper. In Assumption 2.1, by derivative with respect to μ μ we mean the intrinsic derivative, the so-called Wasserstein derivative (see for instance [10, Definition 2.2.2] or [13, Chap. 5] and the discussion therein). In particular, when we say differentiability with respect to the measure variable, we always mean the so-called fully \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^1$$\end{document}C1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^2$$\end{document}C2, etc. classes (see [13, Chap. 5]). In what follows we use the notation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _\mu $$\end{document}∂μ to denote this intrinsic Wasserstein derivative. We denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{x}$$\end{document}x~ the new variable arising after applying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _\mu $$\end{document}∂μ, and we display this after the measure variable as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _\mu g(\mu ,\tilde{x})$$\end{document}∂μg(μ,x~), for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g \in C^1(\mathcal {P}_2(\mathbb {R}^M)).$$\end{document}g∈C1(P2(RM)). Under the prescribed framework, we consider the following optimization problem:

Problem 2.2

Minimizing (5) over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U}^{ad}_t$$\end{document}Utad.

As is mentioned in the introduction, the optimization of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_N$$\end{document}JN under the constraint (4) can be understood as a learning process with neural SDEs/ODEs. For a direct example, consider the case where σ =0σ=0 and the optimal control \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^*_N(t)$$\end{document}θN∗(t) is deterministic, optimizing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_N$$\end{document}JN can be viewed as using neural network to choose the best feedback function of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\big (t,\theta (t),\cdot \big )$$\end{document}f(t,θ(t),·) for optimal control problems according to the sampled objective functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_N$$\end{document}JN, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x_1,\ldots ,x_N)$$\end{document}(x1,…,xN) are the sample inputs drawn from a certain distribution. Here θ (t)θ(t) is the parameter to be determined, which is part of the feedback function. See [37, 38] for similar discrete time models. Please see Sect. 5 for more interpretation on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^*_N(t)$$\end{document}θN∗(t) in another concrete example. Denote the value function to Problem 2.2 by7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} V_N(t,x_1,\ldots ,x_N):=\inf _{\theta \in \mathcal {U}^{ad}_t}J_N(t,\theta ,x_1,\ldots ,x_N), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}VN(t,x1,…,xN):=infθ∈UtadJN(t,θ,x1,…,xN),and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^*_N(t,x_1,\ldots ,x_N)$$\end{document}θN∗(t,x1,…,xN) one of the optimal feedback functions (if exists). Suppose that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {W}_2\bigg (\frac{1}{N}\sum _{k=1}^N\delta _{x_k},\mu \bigg )\ \longrightarrow \ 0\quad \text {as}\quad N\rightarrow +\infty , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}W2(1N∑k=1Nδxk,μ)⟶0asN→+∞,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu \in \mathcal {P}_2(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}μ∈P2(R) is a given probability measure. We are interested in establishing the quantitative convergence results of the value functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_N(t,x_1,\ldots ,x_N)$$\end{document}VN(t,x1,…,xN), optimal feedback functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^*_N(t,x_1,\ldots ,x_N)$$\end{document}θN∗(t,x1,…,xN) as well as optimal parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^*_N(t)=\theta ^*_N\big (t,X^{\theta ^*_N,1}_N(t),\ldots ,X^{\theta ^*_N,N}_N(t)\big )$$\end{document}θN∗(t)=θN∗(t,XNθN∗,1(t),…,XNθN∗,N(t)) when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N\rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}N→+∞. Under suitable assumptions, we give positive answers to these convergence questions in Sect. 4. Towards that end, our first finding is that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_N$$\end{document}VN is the finite projection of a local Lipschitz function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}$$\end{document}V defined on probability space. Such \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}$$\end{document}V is first defined on the set of empirical measures then extended to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}_2(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}P2(R).

Definition 2.1

For samples \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_1,\ldots ,x_N\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x1,…,xN∈R, denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu ^N$$\end{document}μN the corresponding empirical measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu ^N=\frac{1}{N}\sum _{i=1}^N\delta _{x_i}.$$\end{document}μN=1N∑i=1Nδxi. Define8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal{V}(t,\mu ^N):=V_N(t,x_1,\ldots ,x_N). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}V(t,μN):=VN(t,x1,…,xN).

We will show in Lemma 4.1 that Definition 2.1 is meaningful, i.e., if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{1}{N}\sum _{i=1}^N\delta _{x_i}=\frac{1}{M}\sum _{i=1}^M\delta _{y_i}$$\end{document}1N∑i=1Nδxi=1M∑i=1Mδyi for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N,M\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}N,M∈N, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_N(t,x_1,\ldots ,x_N)=V_M(t,y_1,\ldots ,y_M).$$\end{document}VN(t,x1,…,xN)=VM(t,y1,…,yM). Moreover, we show in Sect. 4 that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}$$\end{document}V is locally Lipschitz, which implies the convergence rate of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_N$$\end{document}VN as summarized in Corollary 2.4. The Lipschitz continuity is formulated as follows. We refer to the description of the additional assumptions Hypothesis (R) and Hypothesis (R1) in Sect. 3.2.

Suppose Hypothesis (R). Theorem 2.5

Suppose the same assumptions as in Proposition 3.16. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu ^N=\frac{1}{N}\sum _{i=1}^N\delta _{x_i}$$\end{document}μN=1N∑i=1Nδxi, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu ^N=\frac{1}{N}\sum _{i=1}^N\delta _{y_i}$$\end{document}νN=1N∑i=1Nδyi. Then for T>0T>0,10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |\theta ^*(t,\mu ^N)-\theta ^*(t,\nu ^N)|\le \tilde{C}_9\mathcal {W}_2(\mu ^N,\nu ^N),\ t\in [0,T], \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|θ∗(t,μN)-θ∗(t,νN)|≤C~9W2(μN,νN),t∈[0,T],for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{C}_9=\tilde{C}_9(f,\lambda ^{\frac{1}{2}},T,L,U,(\lambda -\lambda _0)^{-\frac{1}{2}})$$\end{document}C~9=C~9(f,λ12,T,L,U,(λ-λ0)-12).

One implication of the above is the quantitative estimates on optimal feedback functions.

Corollary 2.6

Suppose that the assumptions of Theorem 2.5 take place and suppose that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu ^N:=\frac{1}{N}\sum _{i=1}^N\delta _{x_i}\rightarrow \mu $$\end{document}μN:=1N∑i=1Nδxi→μ in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {P}_2(\mathbb {R}),\mathcal {W}_{2}),$$\end{document}(P2(R),W2), as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N\rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}N→+∞. Then for T>0T>0,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{N\rightarrow +\infty }\theta ^*_N\big (t,x,\nabla _xV_N(t,x)\big )=\theta ^*(t,\mu ),\ t\in [0,T], \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limN→+∞θN∗(t,x,∇xVN(t,x))=θ∗(t,μ),t∈[0,T],at a rate\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\quad |\theta ^*_N\big (t,x,\nabla _xV_N(t,x)\big )-\theta ^*(t,\mu )|\le \tilde{C}_9\mathcal {W}_2(\mu ^N,\mu ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|θN∗(t,x,∇xVN(t,x))-θ∗(t,μ)|≤C~9W2(μN,μ).

For the case where the distribution of samples converge, Theorem 2.5 yields the convergence rate of the empirical measures supported on the optimal path for each sample; and above all, the convergence rate of optimal controls. From the viewpoint of neural networks, such convergence corresponds to the generality of neural networks.

Proof

The proof is the same as in Proposition 4.5. □ □

We also note here that Theorem 2.5, Corollary 2.6 and Proposition 2.7 have short time counter parts that will be discussed in Sect. 4.

The Auxiliary Problems and Corresponding Uniform Estimates

In order to study the aforementioned convergence as well as the convergence rate, we establish uniform derivative estimates on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_N$$\end{document}VN as the number of variables increases, which is different from the usual PDE estimates. Our results include the uniform estimates on the first and the second order derivatives of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_N$$\end{document}VN. These estimates are used in Sect. 4. It turns out (as we will see in the next section) that the estimates on the first order derivatives yield the convergence rate of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_N(t,x_1,\ldots ,x_N)$$\end{document}VN(t,x1,…,xN), while the estimates on the second order derivatives yield the convergence rate of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^*_N(t,x_1,\ldots ,x_N)$$\end{document}θN∗(t,x1,…,xN).

The Auxiliary Problems and the Estimates on the First Order Derivatives

Different from the usual PDE estimates, the estimates here are focused more on the dimension of variables since the dimension, which corresponds to the number of samples, is now changing. We begin with the existence and uniqueness of classical solution to (13).

Lemma 3.1

The HJB Eq. (13) admits a unique bounded solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^{\varepsilon ,R}_N\in C^{1+\frac{\gamma }{2},4+\gamma }_{loc}\left( [0,T)\times \mathbb {R}^N\right) \cap C\left( [0,T]\times \mathbb {R}^N\right) $$\end{document}VNε,R∈Cloc1+γ2,4+γ[0,T)×RN∩C[0,T]×RN where 0<γ <10<γ<1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _tV^{\varepsilon ,R}_N$$\end{document}∂tVNε,R, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _{x_i}V^{\varepsilon ,R}_N$$\end{document}∂xiVNε,R, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial ^2_{x_ix_j}V^{\varepsilon ,R}_N$$\end{document}∂xixj2VNε,R, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le i,j\le N$$\end{document}1≤i,j≤N are bounded.

Proof

Notice that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{R_1}$$\end{document}LR1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U_{R_1}$$\end{document}UR1 as well as their derivatives are all bounded. According to Theorem 4.4.3, Theorem 4.7.2 and Theorem 4.7.4 in [44], the value function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^{\varepsilon ,R}_N$$\end{document}VNε,R defined in (16) is the weak solution (in the distributional sense) to (13), furthermore, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^{\varepsilon ,R}_N$$\end{document}VNε,R and its weak derivatives \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _tV^{\varepsilon ,R}_N$$\end{document}∂tVNε,R, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _{x_i}V^{\varepsilon ,R}_N$$\end{document}∂xiVNε,R, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial ^2_{x_ix_j}V^{\varepsilon ,R}_N$$\end{document}∂xixj2VNε,R, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le i,j\le N$$\end{document}1≤i,j≤N are all bounded. Note also that for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(t,x)\in (0,T)\times \mathbb {R}^N$$\end{document}(t,x)∈(0,T)×RN18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _tV^{\varepsilon ,R}_N(t,x)+\frac{\sigma ^2}{2}\sum _{i,j=1}^N\partial ^2_{x_ix_j}V^{\varepsilon ,R}_N(t,x)+\frac{\varepsilon ^2}{2}\sum _{i=1}^N\partial ^2_{x_ix_i}V^{\varepsilon ,R}_N(t,x)=g(t,x), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tVNε,R(t,x)+σ22∑i,j=1N∂xixj2VNε,R(t,x)+ε22∑i=1N∂xixi2VNε,R(t,x)=g(t,x),where\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} g(t,x):=-\inf _{\theta \in \Theta _{R_2}}\bigg \{\frac{\lambda }{2}\big |\theta \big |^2+\sum _{i=1}^Nf\bigg (t,\theta ,x_i,\frac{1}{N}\sum _{j=1}^N\delta _{x_j}\bigg )\partial _{x_i}V^{\varepsilon ,R}_N+\frac{1}{N}\sum _{i=1}^NL_{R_1}(x_i)\bigg \}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}g(t,x):=-infθ∈ΘR2{λ2|θ|2+∑i=1Nf(t,θ,xi,1N∑j=1Nδxj)∂xiVNε,R+1N∑i=1NLR1(xi)}.As is shown above, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial ^2_{x_ix_j}V^{\varepsilon ,R}_N$$\end{document}∂xixj2VNε,R is bounded. Moreover, we have from Corollary 4.7.8 of [44] that for 0<γ <10<γ<1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla _xV^{\varepsilon ,R}_N(t,x)$$\end{document}∇xVNε,R(t,x) is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\gamma }{2}$$\end{document}γ2-Hölder with respect to t (uniformly in x). Hence g(t, x) is locally Lipschitz continuous with respect to x and Hölder continuous with respect to t. Let us view \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^{\varepsilon ,R}_N$$\end{document}VNε,R as the solution to PDE (18) with constant coefficients, where the terminal conditions are the same as (13). Standard results then yield that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _tV^{\varepsilon ,R}_N$$\end{document}∂tVNε,R, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _{x_i}V^{\varepsilon ,R}_N$$\end{document}∂xiVNε,R, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial ^2_{x_ix_j}V^{\varepsilon ,R}_N\in C^{\frac{\gamma }{2},\gamma }_{loc}\left( [0,T)\times \mathbb {R}^N\right) $$\end{document}∂xixj2VNε,R∈Clocγ2,γ[0,T)×RN, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le i,j\le N$$\end{document}1≤i,j≤N.

As for the uniqueness, we can use the stochastic control interpretation to (13) and show that any solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^{\varepsilon ,R}_N$$\end{document}VNε,R equals the value function in (16) by the standard verification results. □ □

Notice that at the moment the bound on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^{\varepsilon ,R}_N,\ \partial _{x_i}V^{\varepsilon ,R}_N$$\end{document}VNε,R,∂xiVNε,R, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial ^2_{x_ix_j}V^{\varepsilon ,R}_N$$\end{document}∂xixj2VNε,R, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le i,j\le N$$\end{document}1≤i,j≤N might depend on \varepsilon ε, R and N. Before establishing uniform estimates with respect to \varepsilon ε, R and N, we need a refined analysis on the sample path, where the constants are independent of the dimension N of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x=(x_1,\ldots ,x_N)\in \mathbb {R}^N$$\end{document}x=(x1,…,xN)∈RN and the estimates admits a term of averaging due to the mean field effect.

Lemma 3.2

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_i\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}xi∈R, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le i\le N$$\end{document}1≤i≤N, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \in \mathcal {U}^{ad}_{t_0,R_2}$$\end{document}θ∈Ut0,R2ad and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^{\varepsilon ,\theta ,i}_N,$$\end{document}XNε,θ,i, i=1,… ,Ni=1,⋯,N, be the associated sample path in (15). Then there exists a constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{C}_1=\tilde{C}_1(f,T)$$\end{document}C~1=C~1(f,T) (depending only on f, T, independent of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N,\varepsilon ,\sigma ,R_1,R_2$$\end{document}N,ε,σ,R1,R2), increasing in T, such that19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathbb {E}|X^{\varepsilon ,\theta ,i}_N(t)|^2}\le \tilde{C}_1\bigg (1+|x_i|^2+\mathbb {E}\int _{t_0}^T\big |f\big (s,\theta (s),0,\delta _{\{0\}}\big )\big |^2ds+\frac{1}{N}\sum _{j=1}^N|x_j|^2\bigg ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E|XNε,θ,i(t)|2≤C~1(1+|xi|2+E∫t0T|f(s,θ(s),0,δ{0})|2ds+1N∑j=1N|xj|2). And20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\lim _{R_1\rightarrow +\infty }V^{\varepsilon ,R_1}_N(t,x_1,\ldots ,x_N)=V^\varepsilon _N(t,x_1,\ldots ,x_N),\end{aligned}$$\end{document}limR1→+∞VNε,R1(t,x1,…,xN)=VNε(t,x1,…,xN),21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\lim _{\varepsilon \rightarrow 0}V^\varepsilon _N(t,x_1,\ldots ,x_N)=V_N(t,x_1,\ldots ,x_N). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limε→0VNε(t,x1,…,xN)=VN(t,x1,…,xN).

Proof

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_1,\ldots ,x_N\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x1,…,xN∈R, denote by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\tilde{f}_i\big (s,\theta ,x_1,\ldots ,x_N\big ):=f\bigg (s,\theta ,x_i,{\frac{1}{N}\sum _{k=1}^N\delta _{x_k}}\bigg ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f~i(s,θ,x1,…,xN):=f(s,θ,xi,1N∑k=1Nδxk),then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _{x_j}\tilde{f}_i\big (s,\theta ,x_1,\ldots ,x_N\big )&=\delta _{ij}f_x\bigg (s,\theta ,x_i,{\frac{1}{N}\sum _{k=1}^N\delta _{x_k}}\bigg )\\ &\quad +{\frac{1}{N}\partial _\mu f\bigg (s,\theta ,x_i,\frac{1}{N}\sum _{k=1}^N\delta _{x_k},x_j\bigg )}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂xjf~i(s,θ,x1,…,xN)=δijfx(s,θ,xi,1N∑k=1Nδxk)+1N∂μf(s,θ,xi,1N∑k=1Nδxk,xj),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta _{ij}$$\end{document}δij stands for the Kronecker symbol. According to Lemma A.2, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi ^{\pm }_N(s)\in M_N(C_2)$$\end{document}ΦN±(s)∈MN(C2) for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_2=C_2(f,T)$$\end{document}C2=C2(f,T) because \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_N(s)\in M_N(C_1)$$\end{document}AN(s)∈MN(C1). Moreover, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi ^+_N(t)\Phi ^-_N(s)\in M_N(C_2)$$\end{document}ΦN+(t)ΦN-(s)∈MN(C2) due to Lemma A.1. An application of Burkholder–Davis–Gundy inequality (see e.g. [68]) to the i-th component in (23) gives the estimate (19).

Next we turn to (20). Denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^\delta $$\end{document}θδ the δ δ-optimal strategy for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^\varepsilon _N(t,x_1,\ldots ,x_N)$$\end{document}VNε(t,x1,…,xN). In view of (19), the growth condition on L,\ UL,U, and the dominated convergence theorem, we obtain\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \limsup _{R_1\rightarrow +\infty }V^{\varepsilon ,R_1}_N(t,x_1,\ldots ,x_N)&\le \lim _{R_1\rightarrow +\infty }J^{\varepsilon ,R_1}_N(t,\theta ^\delta ,x_1,\ldots ,x_N)\\ &=J^{\varepsilon }_N(t,\theta ,x_1,\ldots ,x_N)\\&\le V^\varepsilon _N(t,x_1,\ldots ,x_N)+\delta . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}lim supR1→+∞VNε,R1(t,x1,…,xN)≤limR1→+∞JNε,R1(t,θδ,x1,…,xN)=JNε(t,θ,x1,…,xN)≤VNε(t,x1,…,xN)+δ.Hence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\limsup _{R_1\rightarrow +\infty }V^{\varepsilon ,R_1}_N(t,x_1,\ldots ,x_N)\le V^\varepsilon _N(t,x_1,\ldots ,x_N).$$\end{document}lim supR1→+∞VNε,R1(t,x1,…,xN)≤VNε(t,x1,…,xN). It remains to show the inverse inequality.

Denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^{R_1,\delta }$$\end{document}θR1,δ the δ δ-optimal strategy for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} ]\\&\quad \ge \frac{\lambda }{2}\mathbb {E}\bigg [\int _{t_0}^T|\theta ^{R_1,\delta }(t)|^2dt\bigg ]-\hat{C}_x, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}JNε,R1(t0,θR1,δ,x1,…,xN)≥E[-CN∑i=1N∫t0T|XNε,θR1,δ,i(t)|2-αdt-CN∑i=1N|XNε,θR1,δ,i(T)|2-α-C+λ∫t0T|θR1,δ(t)|2dt]≥-C(1+|xi|2+E∫t0T|f(s,θR1,δ(s),0,δ{0})|2ds+1N∑j=1N|xj|2)2-α2+E[λ∫t0T|θR1,δ(t)|2dt]≥-E[C∫t0T|θR1,δ(t)|2dt]2-α2-Cx+E[λ∫t0T|θR1,δ(t)|2dt]≥λ2E[∫t0T|θR1,δ(t)|2dt]-C^x,where
