Hence\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\lim _{R_1\rightarrow +\infty }\mathbb {E}\bigg [\frac{1}{N}\sum _{i=1}^N\int _s^T|L_{R_1}-L|\big (X^{\varepsilon ,\theta ^{R_1,\delta },i}_N(t)\big )dt+\frac{1}{N}\sum _{i=1}^N|U_{R_1}\\ &\quad -U|\big (X^{\varepsilon ,\theta ^{R_1,\delta },i}_N(T)\big )\bigg ]=0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limR1→+∞E[1N∑i=1N∫sT|LR1-L|(XNε,θR1,δ,i(t))dt+1N∑i=1N|UR1-U|(XNε,θR1,δ,i(T))]=0.Therefore\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\delta +\lim _{R_1\rightarrow +\infty }V^{\varepsilon ,R_1}_N(t,x_1,\ldots ,x_N)\ge \liminf _{R_1\rightarrow +\infty }J^{\varepsilon ,R_1}_N(s,\theta ^{R_1,\delta },x_1,\ldots ,x_N)\\&\quad =\liminf _{R_1\rightarrow +\infty }J^{\varepsilon }_N(s,\theta ^{R_1,\delta },x_1,\ldots ,x_N)\\&\quad \ge V^\varepsilon _N(t,x_1,\ldots ,x_N). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}δ+limR1→+∞VNε,R1(t,x1,…,xN)≥lim _N(t,x_1,\ldots ,x_N) \le \delta +\lim _{R_1\rightarrow +\infty }V^{\varepsilon ,R_1}_N(t,x_1,\ldots ,x_N) \le \delta + V^\varepsilon _N(t,x_1,\ldots ,x_N). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}VNε(t,x1,…,xN)≤δ+limR1→+∞VNε,R1(t,x1,…,xN)≤δ+VNε(t,x1,…,xN).Thus, by the arbitrariness of δ δ we obtained\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \lim _{R_1\rightarrow +\infty }V^{\varepsilon ,R_1}_N(t,x_1,\ldots ,x_N) = V^\varepsilon _N(t,x_1,\ldots ,x_N), $$\end{document}limR1→+∞VNε,R1(t,x1,…,xN)=VNε(t,x1,…,xN),for any convergent subsequence. So, the convergence does not depend on the particular subsequence ant it must take place for the full family \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(V^{\varepsilon ,R_1}_N(t,x_1,\ldots ,x_N))_{R_1>0}$$\end{document}(VNε,R1(t,x1,…,xN))R1>0.

Hence (20) follows. The equality (21) can be obtained using the similar approach and estimates to the above. □ □

Remark 3.3

Take \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta (t)\equiv 0$$\end{document}θ(t)≡0 (which is admissible since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\in \Theta $$\end{document}0∈Θ), then (19) can be rephrased as24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathbb {E}|X^{\varepsilon ,0,i}_N(t)|^2}\le \tilde{C}_1\bigg (1+|x_i|^2+\frac{1}{N}\sum _{j=1}^N|x_j|^2\bigg ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E|XNε,0,i(t)|2≤C~1(1+|xi|2+1N∑j=1N|xj|2).

Based on Lemma 3.2, we can go on with the estimates on the first derivatives. In the context below, the values of constants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_k$$\end{document}Ck, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{C}_k$$\end{document}C~k, k≥ 1k≥1, might vary, but their dependence on the model parameters remains the same.

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(t,p,q,z,\theta )\in [0,T]\times \mathbb {R}^N\times \mathbb {R}^N\times \mathbb {R}^{N\times N}\times \mathbb {R}^N$$\end{document}(t,p,q,z,θ)∈[0,T]×RN×RN×RN×N×RN, define the following Hamiltonian25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H^{R_1}_N(t,x,p,\theta )&:=\lambda \big |\theta \big |^2+\sum _{i=1}^Nf\bigg (t,\theta ,x_i,{\frac{1}{N}\sum _{j=1}^N\delta _{x_j}}\bigg )p_i+\frac{1}{N}\sum _{i=1}^NL_{R_1}(x_i), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HN1(t,x,p,θ):=λ|θ|2+∑i=1Nf(t,θ,xi,1N∑j=1Nδxj)pi+1N∑i=1NLR1(xi),as well as, for later use,26\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_N(t,x,p,\theta )&:=\lambda \big |\theta \big |^2+\sum _{i=1}^Nf\bigg (t,\theta ,x_i,{\frac{1}{N}\sum _{j=1}^N\delta _{x_j}}\bigg )p_i+\frac{1}{N}\sum _{i=1}^NL(x_i). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HN(t,x,p,θ):=λ|θ|2+∑i=1Nf(t,θ,xi,1N∑j=1Nδxj)pi+1N∑i=1NL(xi).With the preparation above, we show the following estimates on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^{\varepsilon ,R}_N$$\end{document}VNε,R in (13).

Lemma 3.4

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_i\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}xi∈R, t∈ [0,T],t∈[0,T], \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le i\le N$$\end{document}1≤i≤N and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^{\varepsilon ,R}_N$$\end{document}VNε,R be the solution to (13). Then there exists a constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{C}_2=\tilde{C}_2(f,\lambda ^{-\frac{1}{2}},T)$$\end{document}C~2=C~2(f,λ-12,T), increasing in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T,\lambda ^{-\frac{1}{2}}$$\end{document}T,λ-12, independent of N, σ σ, \varepsilon ε and R such that for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le i\le N,$$\end{document}1≤i≤N,27\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \big |\partial _{x_i}V^{\varepsilon ,R}_N(t,x)\big |&\le \frac{\tilde{C}_2(C^L_{11}+C^U_{11})}{N}\bigg (1+|x_i|^2+\frac{1}{N}\sum _{j=1}^N|x_j|^2\bigg )^\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\tilde{C}_2(C^L_{10}+C^U_{10})}{N}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|∂xiVNε,R(t,x)|≤C~2(C11L+C11U)N(1+|xi|2+1N∑j=1N|xj|2)12+C~2(C10L+C10U)N.

Proof

It suffices to show the existence of such \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{C}_2$$\end{document}C^2 for t=0t=0. For other t∈ [0,T]t∈[0,T] the proof and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{C}_2$$\end{document}C^2 can be deduced in the same way.

In view of Lemma 3.1, the HJB Eq. (13) admits a classical solution. Following the standard verification procedure (see e.g. [30]), one can show the existence of an optimal control (in the weak sense) and the corresponding optimal path. Hence we may denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^*(t)$$\end{document}θ∗(t) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(X^*_N(t),Y^*_N(t))$$\end{document}(XN∗(t),YN∗(t)) the optimal control, optimal path as well as the adjoint process. Proof

We recall Lemma 3.1 saying that (13) admits classical solutions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^{\varepsilon ,R}_N$$\end{document}VNε,R. The Estimates on the Second Order Derivatives

In this section we establish uniform estimates on the second order derivatives \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial ^2_{x_ix_j}V^{\varepsilon ,R_1}_N$$\end{document}∂xixj2VNε,R1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le i,j\le N$$\end{document}1≤i,j≤N for the solution to (40) where the parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_2$$\end{document}R2 has been dropped. To do so, our idea is to formally take the derivatives with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_i$$\end{document}xi and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_j$$\end{document}xj in (40) and obtain the PDE system on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial ^2_{x_ix_j}V^{\varepsilon ,R_1}_N$$\end{document}∂xixj2VNε,R1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le i,j\le N$$\end{document}1≤i,j≤N. The above differentiation requires further analysis on the differentiability of Hamiltonian in (40). It then turns out that the aforementioned analysis involves the uniform estimates on the first order derivatives in (42). We can see from (42) that the first order derivatives therein are only locally bounded in general. In our forthcoming analysis, we propose some technical assumptions so as to deal with this non-global boundedness.

Lastly, such a formulation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}_{N,\Gamma _1,\Gamma _2}$$\end{document}AN,Γ1,Γ2 will serve for a more flexible description of the ‘localized’ properties on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big \{\big (x,\nabla _xV^{\varepsilon ,R_1}_N(t,x)\big ):(t,x)\in [0,T]\times \mathbb {R}^N\big \}$$\end{document}{(x,∇xVNε,R1(t,x)):(t,x)∈[0,T]×RN} in Lemma 3.9 and Proposition 3.16 below.

We also note that if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial ^2_{\theta \theta }f\ge 0$$\end{document}∂θθ2f≥0, then any λ >0λ>0 will fulfil Hypothesis (R) when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Gamma _1,\Gamma _2)=(0,\tilde{C}_2)$$\end{document}(Γ1,Γ2)=(0,C~2). Lastly, such a formulation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}_{N,\Gamma _1,\Gamma _2}$$\end{document}AN,Γ1,Γ2 will serve for a more flexible description of the ‘localized’ properties on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big \{\big (x,\nabla _xV^{\varepsilon ,R_1}_N(t,x)\big ):(t,x)\in [0,T]\times \mathbb {R}^N\big \}$$\end{document}{(x,∇xVNε,R1(t,x)):(t,x)∈[0,T]×RN} in Lemma 3.9 and Proposition 3.16 below.

For an LQ model with uncontrolled diffusion, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial ^2_{x\theta }f,\partial ^2_{xx}f,\partial ^2_{x\mu }f,\partial ^2_{\theta \mu }f=0$$\end{document}∂xθ2f,∂xx2f,∂xμ2f,∂θμ2f=0 and (46), (47) holds trivially.

For an LQ model with uncontrolled diffusion, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial ^2_{x\theta }f,\partial ^2_{xx}f,\partial ^2_{x\mu }f,\partial ^2_{\theta \mu }f=0$$\end{document}∂xθ2f,∂xx2f,∂xμ2f,∂θμ2f=0 and (46), (47) holds trivially.

For f, L, U with bounded derivatives, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^L_{11}+C^U_{11}=0$$\end{document}C11L+C11U=0 and (46), (47) holds trivially.

For f, L, U with bounded derivatives, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^L_{11}+C^U_{11}=0$$\end{document}C11L+C11U=0 and (46), (47) holds trivially.

Lemma 3.7

Suppose that Hypothesis (R) with parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma _1,\ \Gamma _2$$\end{document}Γ1,Γ2 in (44) takes place. Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^{R_1}_N(t,x,p)$$\end{document}θN1(t,x,p) is locally Lipschitz continuous with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x,p)\in \mathcal {A}_{N, \Gamma _1,\Gamma _2}$$\end{document}(x,p)∈AN,Γ1,Γ2 with derivatives49\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \big |\partial _{x_k}\theta ^{R_1}_N(t,x,p)\big |&\le \frac{{(\lambda -\lambda _0)^{-1}}C^Q}{N},\ \big |\partial _{p_k}\theta ^{R_1}_N(t,x,p)\big |\nonumber \\ &\le {(\lambda -\lambda _0)^{-1}}\Vert f_\theta \Vert _\infty ,\ k=1,\ldots ,N. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|∂xkθN1(t,x,p)|≤(λ-λ0)-1CQN,|∂pkθN1(t,x,p)|≤(λ-λ0)-1‖fθ‖∞,k=1,…,N.

Proof

We postpone the proof of this result to Appendix A. □ □

Lemma 3.8

Suppose that the assumptions of Lemma 3.7 take place. Then there exists a constant\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\tilde{C}_3=\tilde{C}_3(f,\lambda ^{\frac{1}{2}},T,L,(\lambda -\lambda _0)^{-1})}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}C~3=C~3(f,λ12,T,L,(λ-λ0)-1),increasing in T, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\lambda ^{-\frac{1}{2}},\ (\lambda -\lambda _0)^{-1}}$$\end{document}λ-12,(λ-λ0)-1, such that for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x,p)\in \mathcal {A}_N$$\end{document}(x,p)∈AN,50\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\big |\partial _{x_i}\tilde{H}^{R_1}_N(t,x,p)\big |,\big |\partial ^2_{x_ip_j}\tilde{H}^{R_1}_N(t,x,p)\big |\le \tilde{C}_3N^{-1},\quad \big |\partial ^2_{x_ix_j}\tilde{H}^{R_1}_N(t,x,p)\big |\nonumber \\&\quad \le \tilde{C}_3N^{-1}(\delta _{ij}+N^{-1}),\nonumber \\&\big |\partial _{p_i}\tilde{H}^{R_1}_N( t,x,p)\big |,\big |\partial ^2_{p_ip_j}\tilde{H}^{R_1}_N(t,x,p)\big |\le \tilde{C}_3,\quad 1\le i,j\le N. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|∂xiH~N1(t,x,p)|,|∂xipj2H~N1(t,x,p)|≤C~3N-1,|∂xixj2H~N1(t,x,p)|≤C~3N-1(δij+N-1),|∂piH~N1(t,x,p)|,|∂pipj2H~N1(t,x,p)|≤C~3,1≤i,j≤N.

Proof

Recall (41) and (48),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tilde{H}^{R_1}_N(t,x,p)=H^{R_1}_N\big (t,x,p,\theta ^{R_1}_N(t,x,p)\big ),\quad (x,p)\in \mathcal {A}_N. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}H~N1(t,x,p)=HN1(t,x,p,θN1(t,x,p)),(x,p)∈AN.Hence we can obtain the above estimates via (49). □ □

Short Time Estimates

As is mentioned before, with the preparation above, we may take partial derivatives in (40) and derive the equation satisfied by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^{\varepsilon ,kl}_N:=\partial ^2_{x_kx_l}V^{\varepsilon ,R_1}_N$$\end{document}VNε,kl:=∂xkxl2VNε,R1. We begin with a regularity results which validates the differentiation.

Lemma 3.9

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma ^0_1,\ \Gamma ^0_2\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}Γ10,Γ20∈R be given constants such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (x,\nabla _xV^{\varepsilon ,R_1}_N(t,x)\big )\in \mathcal {A}_{N,\Gamma ^0_1,\Gamma ^0_2}$$\end{document}(x,∇xVNε,R1(t,x))∈AN,Γ10,Γ20 for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon ,R_1>0,\ (t,x)\in [0,T]\times \mathbb {R}^N$$\end{document}ε,R1>0,(t,x)∈[0,T]×RN. Suppose that Hypothesis (R) holds with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Gamma _1,\Gamma _2)=(\Gamma ^0_1,\Gamma ^0_2)$$\end{document}(Γ1,Γ2)=(Γ10,Γ20) in (44). The Eq. (40) admits a unique classical solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^{\varepsilon ,R_1}_N\in C\left( [0,T]\times \mathbb {R}^N\right) $$\end{document}VNε,R1∈C[0,T]×RN where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^{\varepsilon ,R_1}_N, \partial _{x_i}V^{\varepsilon ,R_1}_N,\partial ^2_{x_ix_j}V^{\varepsilon ,R_1}_N, 1\le i,j\le N$$\end{document}VNε,R1,∂xiVNε,R1,∂xixj2VNε,R1,1≤i,j≤N are bounded. Moreover for 0<γ <10<γ<1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^{\varepsilon ,R_1}_N, \partial _{x_i}V^{\varepsilon ,R_1}_N,\partial ^2_{x_ix_j}V^{\varepsilon ,R_1}_N\in C^{1+\frac{\gamma }{2},2+\gamma }_{loc}\left( [0,T)\times \mathbb {R}^N\right) , 1\le i,j\le N$$\end{document}VNε,R1,∂xiVNε,R1,∂xixj2VNε,R1∈Cloc1+γ2,2+γ[0,T)×RN,1≤i,j≤N.

Proof

In Lemma 3.5 we have shown that the solution to (40) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^{\varepsilon ,R_1}_N\in C([0,T]\times \mathbb {R}^N)$$\end{document}VNε,R1∈C([0,T]×RN) has bounded derivatives \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^{\varepsilon ,R_1}_N, \partial _{x_i}V^{\varepsilon ,R_1}_N,\partial ^2_{x_ix_j}V^{\varepsilon ,R_1}_N, 1\le i,j\le N$$\end{document}VNε,R1,∂xiVNε,R1,∂xixj2VNε,R1,1≤i,j≤N. In order to show the higher regularity of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^{\varepsilon ,R_1}_N$$\end{document}VNε,R1, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_2$$\end{document}R2 be sufficiently large and take \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _{x_i}$$\end{document}∂xi (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le i\le N$$\end{document}1≤i≤N) in (18) to obtain the linear PDE satisfied by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _{x_i}V^{\varepsilon ,R_1}_N$$\end{document}∂xiVNε,R1. Repeat the above procedure, we can show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y^*(t)=Y^{**}(t)$$\end{document}Y∗(t)=Y∗∗(t) on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in [T-\delta ,T]$$\end{document}t∈[T-δ,T], \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[T-2\delta ,T-\delta ]$$\end{document}[T-2δ,T-δ] and after finite times repetitions we obtain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y^*(t)=Y^{**}(t)$$\end{document}Y∗(t)=Y∗∗(t) on t∈ [0,T]t∈[0,T]. The uniqueness above thus tells us that Y in (54) is the only bounded fixed point of Φ Φ.

Global in Time Estimates

In this subsection we focus on the global estimates for any given T>0T>0 with sufficiently smooth data. As will be seen, the global estimates rely heavily on the convexity assumption (with respect to x) on the Hamiltonian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{H}_N(t,x,p)$$\end{document}H~N(t,x,p) in (60). However, the truncation of L, U might break the convexity of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{H}_N(t,x,p)$$\end{document}H~N(t,x,p). Therefore, we need to pass \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_1$$\end{document}R1 to infinity in (40) and consider59\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\{ \begin{aligned}&\partial _tV^\varepsilon _N+\frac{\sigma ^2}{2}\sum _{i,j=1}^N\partial ^2_{x_ix_j}V^\varepsilon _N+\frac{\varepsilon ^2}{2}\sum _{i=1}^N\partial ^2_{x_ix_i}V^\varepsilon _N+\tilde{H}_N(t,x,\nabla _xV^\varepsilon _N)=0,\\&V^\varepsilon _N(T,x_1,\ldots ,x_N)=\frac{1}{N}\sum _{i=1}^NU(x_i), \end{aligned}\right. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tVNε+σ22∑i,j=1N∂xixj2VNε+ε22∑i=1N∂xixi2VNε+H~N(t,x,∇xVNε)=0,VNε(T,x1,…,xN)=1N∑i=1NU(xi),Here60\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tilde{H}_N(t,x,p):=\inf _{\theta \in \mathbb {R}}\bigg \{\frac{\lambda }{2}\big |\theta \big |^2+\sum _{i=1}^Nf\bigg (t,\theta ,x_i,\frac{1}{N}\sum _{j=1}^N\delta _{x_j}\bigg )p_i+\frac{1}{N}\sum _{i=1}^NL(x_i)\bigg \}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}H~N(t,x,p):=infθ∈R{λ2|θ|2+∑i=1Nf(t,θ,xi,1N∑j=1Nδxj)pi+1N∑i=1NL(xi)}.Similarly to (51), we would like to take \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial ^2_{x_kx_l}$$\end{document}∂xkxl2 in (59) and analysis the resulting system. To do so, we show the validity of taking derivatives in the next proposition.

Proposition 3.11

Suppose that the assumptions of Lemma 3.9 take place. As a result, for the first order derivatives of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^{\varepsilon ,R_1}_N$$\end{document}VNε,R1, we also have62\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \big |\partial _{x_i}V^\varepsilon _N(t,x)\big |&\le \frac{\tilde{C}_2(C^L_{11}+C^U_{11})}{N}\bigg (1+|x_i|^2+\frac{1}{N}\sum _{j=1}^N|x_j|^2\bigg )^\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\tilde{C}_2(C^L_{10}+C^U_{10})}{N}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|∂xiVNε(t,x)|≤C~2(C11L+C11U)N(1+|xi|2+1N∑j=1N|xj|2)12+C~2(C10L+C10U)N,where the constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{C}_2$$\end{document}C~2 is from Lemma 3.4.

Remark 3.12

As we will see in the analysis below, the second assumption in Hypothesis (R1) on the convexity of the Hamiltonian in the x-variable can be relaxed as eventually it will be used only restricted to the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\{ (t,x,p)\in [0,T]\times \mathbb {R}^N\times \mathbb {R}^N: p = \nabla _xV^\varepsilon _N(t,x)\right\} .$$\end{document}(t,x,p)∈[0,T]×RN×RN:p=∇xVNε(t,x). Such relaxation will also be shown to be particularly meaningful in the case study of Sect. 5. The assumption on the convexity of the Hamiltonian is quite common in mean field control problems. In general, infinite dimensional HJB equations for mean field control are degenerate despite the presence of non-degenerate noise, and convexity conditions are used to establish the a priori estimates for global classical solutions. In our context, the convexity condition is used to established uniform in N estimates in Lemma 3.14–Proposition 3.16 below. In our model, since the control is centralized and the dynamics of the particles are more complicated, this assumption could no longer be guaranteed in a simple way. According to direct calculation, with the notation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu =\frac{1}{N}\sum _{j=1}^N\delta _{x_j}$$\end{document}μ=1N∑j=1Nδxj, we find\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial ^2_{x_kx_l}\tilde{H}_N(t,x,p)&=\delta _{kl}\frac{1}{N}L''(x_k)+\partial ^2_{\theta x}f(t,\theta ^*,x_k,\mu )p_k\partial _{x_l}\theta ^*\nonumber \\ &\quad +\delta _{kl}\partial ^2_{xx}f(t,\theta ^*,x_k,\mu )p_k\\&\quad +\frac{1}{N}\partial ^2_{x\mu }f(t,\theta ^*,x_k,\mu ,x_l)p_k\nonumber \\&\quad +\frac{1}{N}\partial ^2_{x\mu }f(t,\theta ^*,x_l,\mu ,x_k)p_l\nonumber \\ &\quad +\frac{1}{N}\sum _{i=1}^N\partial ^2_{\mu \theta }f(t,\theta ^*,x_i,\mu ,x_k)p_i\partial _{x_l}\theta ^*\nonumber \\&\quad +\delta _{kl}\frac{1}{N}\sum _{i=1}^N\partial ^2_{\mu \tilde{x}}f(t,\theta ^*,x_i,\mu ,x_k)p_i\\&\quad +\frac{1}{N^2}\sum _{i=1}^N\partial ^2_{\mu \mu }f(t,\theta ^*,x_i,\mu ,x_k,x_l)p_i. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂xkxl2H~N(t,x,p)=δkl1NL′′(xk)+∂θx2f(t,θ∗,xk,μ)pk∂xlθ∗+δkl∂xx2f(t,θ∗,xk,μ)pk+1N∂xμ2f(t,θ∗,xk,μ,xl)pk+1N∂xμ2f(t,θ∗,xl,μ,xk)pl+1N∑i=1N∂μθ2f(t,θ∗,xi,μ,xk)pi∂xlθ∗+δkl1N∑i=1N∂μx~2f(t,θ∗,xi,μ,xk)pi+1N2∑i=1N∂μμ2f(t,θ∗,xi,μ,xk,xl)pi.We can see from the above that one possible way to ensure the convexity of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{H}_N(t,x,p)$$\end{document}H~N(t,x,p) in x is to assume an affine structure on f.

Indeed, set the parameters in (4) and (5) as follows\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {f\left( t,\theta ,x,\mu \right) =\theta +x+\int _{\mathbb {R}}y\mu (dy)},\ { L''(x)\ge 0,\ U''(x)\ge 0},\ \forall x\in \mathbb {R},\ \sigma =\lambda =1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ft,θ,x,μ=θ+x+∫Ryμ(dy),L′′(x)≥0,U′′(x)≥0,∀x∈R,σ=λ=1.Then Hypothesis (R1) can be easily verified. A more sophisticated example that fulfils Hypothesis (R1) will be analyzed in Sect. 5, where f is nonlinear and L, U are increasing.

We also note here that the convexity condition (64) is the analogy of displacement monotonicity in mean field games. Inspired by the literature on mean field games, there are at least two possible ways to relax the requirement on convexity. One possible way is to consider linear quadratic framework, see [49] where mean field games of controls with non-monotonic data is studied. The other possible way is to introduce the λ λ-monotonicity condition or involve canonical transformations, see [1, 2, 52, 55].

Similar to the last subsection, the key estimate in this subsection is from the BSDE of Riccati type (70) below. The following lemma is devoted to estimating the terms appearing in (70).

Lemma 3.13

Suppose Hypothesis (R1) with certain parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma _1,\ \Gamma _2$$\end{document}Γ1,Γ2 in (44), then for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\big |\partial _{x_i}\tilde{H}_N\big (t,x,\nabla _xV^\varepsilon _N(t,x)\big )\big |,\big |\partial ^2_{x_ip_j}\tilde{H}_N\big (t,x,\nabla _xV^\varepsilon _N(t,x)\big )\big |\le \tilde{C}_3N^{-1},\nonumber \\&\big |\partial ^2_{x_ix_j}\tilde{H}_N\big (t,x,\nabla _xV^\varepsilon _N(t,x)\big )\big |\le \tilde{C}_3N^{-1}(\delta _{ij}+N^{-1}),\nonumber \\&\big Proof

In view of Lemma 3.8, the constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{C}_3$$\end{document}C~3 in (50) is independent of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_1$$\end{document}R1. Therefore we can let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_1$$\end{document}R1 go to infinity and obtain (65) according to definitions in (41) and (60). Furthermore, in view of the second inequality in (65), we can deduce the existence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{C}_5$$\end{document}C~5 such that for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi \in \mathbb {R}^N$$\end{document}ξ∈RN, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(t,x)\in [0,T]\times \mathbb {R}^N$$\end{document}(t,x)∈[0,T]×RN,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\sum _{i,j=1}^N\partial ^2_{x_ix_j}\tilde{H}_N\big (t,x,\nabla _xV^\varepsilon _N(t,x)\big )\xi _i\xi _j\le \frac{1}{2}\sum _{i,j=1}^N\partial ^2_{x_ix_j}\tilde{H}_N\big (t,x,\nabla _xV^\varepsilon _N(t,x)\big )(\xi ^2_i+\xi ^2_j)\\&\quad \le \frac{\tilde{C}_5}{N}|\xi |^2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑i,j=1N∂xixj2H~N(t,x,∇xVNε(t,x))ξiξj≤12∑i,j=1N∂xixj2H~N(t,x,∇xVNε(t,x))(ξi2+ξj2)≤C~5N|ξ|2. Combining the above with Hypothesis (R1) we have the last inequality. □ □

With the preparation above, if we further assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial ^2_{x_ix_j}V^\varepsilon _N(t,x)$$\end{document}∂xixj2VNε(t,x) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1\le i,j\le N)$$\end{document}(1≤i,j≤N) are bounded, we would then obtain a refined estimate on the bound of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial ^2_{x_ix_j}V^\varepsilon _N(t,x)$$\end{document}∂xixj2VNε(t,x) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1\le i,j\le N)$$\end{document}(1≤i,j≤N) in (66). This is obtained via the BSDE of Riccati type in (70) below where the convexity assumption plays a key role.

Remark 3.15

To see why we confine ourselves to the case where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial ^2_{x_ix_j}V^\varepsilon _N(t,x)$$\end{document}∂xixj2VNε(t,x) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1\le i,j\le N)$$\end{document}(1≤i,j≤N) are bounded, one might turn to the definition of the matrix valued process \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _t$$\end{document}Φt. If we do not assume that Y(t) is bounded, then we cannot ensure the integrability of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _t$$\end{document}Φt. Without the integrability of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _t$$\end{document}Φt, we can not do the calculations in (70) and below, since they all involve taking conditional expectation.For now, in this Lemma 3.14, the existence of the constants δ δ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\breve{C}$$\end{document}C˘ is merely an assumption. But we know from Proposition 3.10 and Proposition 3.11 that δ δ indeed exists and is at least \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{c}$$\end{document}c~, so does \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\breve{C}$$\end{document}C˘. In the next Proposition 3.16, we will use the refined estimate (66) to show that δ =Tδ=T. Moreover, showing that δ =Tδ=T will then in turn gives us the refined estimate (66) on [0, T].

To see why we confine ourselves to the case where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial ^2_{x_ix_j}V^\varepsilon _N(t,x)$$\end{document}∂xixj2VNε(t,x) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1\le i,j\le N)$$\end{document}(1≤i,j≤N) are bounded, one might turn to the definition of the matrix valued process \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _t$$\end{document}Φt. If we do not assume that Y(t) is bounded, then we cannot ensure the integrability of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _t$$\end{document}Φt. To see why we confine ourselves to the case where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial ^2_{x_ix_j}V^\varepsilon _N(t,x)$$\end{document}∂xixj2VNε(t,x) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1\le i,j\le N)$$\end{document}(1≤i,j≤N) are bounded, one might turn to the definition of the matrix valued process \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _t$$\end{document}Φt. We finish this section with the next proposition where the extra assumption on boundedness in Lemma 3.14 is removed. The main idea is to take advantage of the refined estimate in (66) while utilizing a suitable ‘continuity’ method.

Proposition 3.16

Let constants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma ^0_1,\ \Gamma ^0_2>0$$\end{document}Γ10,Γ20>0 be such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (x,\nabla _xV^\varepsilon _N(t,x)\big )\in \mathcal {A}_{N,\Gamma ^0_1,\Gamma ^0_2}$$\end{document}(x,∇xVNε(t,x))∈AN,Γ10,Γ20 for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon >0,\ (t,x)\in [0,T]\times \mathbb {R}^N$$\end{document}ε>0,(t,x)∈[0,T]×RN. Suppose that Hypothesis (R1) holds with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Gamma _1,\Gamma _2)=(\Gamma ^0_1,\Gamma ^0_2)$$\end{document}(Γ1,Γ2)=(Γ10,Γ20). Proof

Convergence of Auxiliary Problems

In this section, we study the original problem associated to the HJB Eq. (12). Thanks to the uniform estimates in the previous sections, we may obtain the desired solution by extracting a convergent subsequence from the families \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(V^{\varepsilon ,R_1}_N)_{{\varepsilon ,R_{1}}}$$\end{document}(VNε,R1)ε,R1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(V^{\varepsilon }_N)_{\varepsilon }$$\end{document}(VNε)ε which solve (40) and (59), respectively. More importantly, the resulting limits inherit the estimates (uniform in N) satisfied by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^{\varepsilon ,R_1}_N$$\end{document}VNε,R1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^{\varepsilon }_N$$\end{document}VNε.

For short time, we have the following result on the well-posedness of (12) as well as the corresponding estimates.

Theorem 3.17

Suppose that the assumptions of Lemma 3.9 take place. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{c}>0$$\end{document}c~>0 given in Proposition 3.10. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T<\tilde{c}$$\end{document}T<c~, the original HJB Eq. (12) admits a solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_N\in W^{1,2,\infty }_{loc}([0,T]\times \mathbb {R}^N)$$\end{document}VN∈Wloc1,2,∞([0,T]×RN), satisfying for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le i,j\le N$$\end{document}1≤i,j≤N, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} The local Lipschitz continuity then gives the strong uniqueness of the solution. Notice that we have got (77), the weak existence can be deduced from (79) and from the linear growth property that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \bigg |f\bigg (t,\theta ^*_N(t,x,\nabla _xV_N(t,x)),x_1,\frac{1}{N}\sum _{j=1}^N\rho (x_j)\bigg )\bigg |\le C_N(1+|x|), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|f(t,θN∗(t,x,∇xVN(t,x)),x1,1N∑j=1Nρ(xj))|≤CN(1+|x|),for some constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_N$$\end{document}CN.

Having shown the well-posedness of (85), the first “≤ ≤” in (82) becomes “==”. The estimates in (78) then enable us to replace the “≤ ≤” in (84) with “≥ ≥”, implying that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^*_N$$\end{document}θN∗ is optimal and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_N$$\end{document}VN is the value function. □ □

Using the same method as in Theorem 3.17 and combining with the uniform estimates in Propositions 3.11, 3.16, we can prove the following result for long time.

Theorem 3.18

Suppose that the assumptions of Proposition 3.16 take place. The original HJB Eq. (12) admits a solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} An optimal feedback function is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \theta ^*_N\big (t,x\big )\in \arg \min _{\theta \in \Theta }\bigg \{\frac{\lambda }{2}\big |\theta \big |^2+\sum _{i=1}^Nf\bigg (t,\theta ,x_i,\frac{1}{N}\sum _{j=1}^N\delta _{x_j})\bigg )\partial _{x_i}V_N(t,x)\bigg \}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}θN∗(t,x)∈argminθ∈Θ{λ2|θ|2+∑i=1Nf(t,θ,xi,1N∑j=1Nδxj))∂xiVN(t,x)}.

Discussion on the Convergence Rate

In this section we discuss the convergence rate for the value functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_N$$\end{document}VN as well as the minimizer \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^*_N$$\end{document}θN∗ where the number of samples N goes to infinity. In terms of neural SDEs, the convergence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_N$$\end{document}VN above is instantly interpreted as the convergence of minima of objective functionals, while we may use the convergence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^*_N$$\end{document}θN∗ above to yield pathwise convergence results that imply the convergence of optimal parameters obtained via neural SDE with N samples (see Propositions 2.7 and 4.5 below). We recall that for sufficiently large N, the conclusion in Theorem 3.17 holds as long as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T<\tilde{c}$$\end{document}T<c~, while the conclusion in Theorem 3.18 holds for any T>0T>0.

We first show the interesting fact that the value function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_N$$\end{document}VN of Problem 2.2 is actually the finite dimensional projection of a function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{V}$$\end{document}V defined on the set of probability measure. The following lemma implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}$$\end{document}V in Definition 2.1 is well-defined.

Lemma 4.1

Suppose Hypothesis (R). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_N$$\end{document}VN be the value function in (7). For samples \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_1,\ldots ,x_N\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x1,…,xN∈R and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y_1,\ldots ,y_M\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}y1,…,yM∈R, (for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M,N\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}M,N∈N) suppose that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{1}{N}\sum _{i=1}^N\delta _{x_i}=\frac{1}{M}\sum _{i=1}^M\delta _{y_i}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}1N∑i=1Nδxi=1M∑i=1Mδyi,then for t∈ [0,T]t∈[0,T], T>0T>0,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} V_N(t,x_1,\ldots ,x_N)=V_M(t,y_1,\ldots ,y_M). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}VN(t,x1,…,xN)=VM(t,y1,…,yM).

Proof of Theorem 2.3

Lemma 4.2

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_N$$\end{document}VN be the value function in (7). Proof

Similarly to the comments before (89), it suffices to show that95\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sum _{i=1}^N\varphi (x_i)\partial _{x_i}V_N(t,x_1,\ldots ,x_N)=\sum _{i=1}^{MN}\varphi (y_i)\partial _{y_i}V_{NM}(t,y_1,\ldots ,y_{NM}), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑i=1Nφ(xi)∂xiVN(t,x1,…,xN)=∑i=1MNφ(yi)∂yiVNM(t,y1,…,yNM),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(y_1,\ldots ,y_{NM})=(x_1{\textbf {1}}^\top _M,\ldots ,x_N{\textbf {1}}^\top _M)$$\end{document}(y1,…,yNM)=(x11M⊤,…,xN1M⊤), in other words, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y_{(i-1)+k}=x_i$$\end{document}y(i-1)+k=xi, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le i\le N$$\end{document}1≤i≤N. According to Lemma 4.1,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} V_N(t,x_1,\ldots ,x_N)=V_{NM}(t,x_1{\textbf {1}}^\top _M,\ldots ,x_N{\textbf {1}}^\top _M)=V_{NM}(t,y_1,\ldots ,y_{NM}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}VN(t,x1,…,xN)=VNM(t,x11M⊤,…,xN1M⊤)=VNM(t,y1,…,yNM).Take the weak derivative with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_i$$\end{document}xi and obtain\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _{x_i}V_N(t,x_1,\ldots ,x_N)=\sum _{k=1}^M\partial _{y_{(i-1)+k}}V_{NM}(t,y_1,\ldots ,y_{NM}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂xiVN(t,x1,…,xN)=∑k=1M∂y(i-1)+kVNM(t,y1,…,yNM).Using the equality above and noticing that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y_{(i-1)+k}=x_i$$\end{document}y(i-1)+k=xi, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le i\le N$$\end{document}1≤i≤N, we can show that (95) is true.

To show (94), we may plug in (8) and (92). Then (94) and (93) follows. □ □

According to Lemma 4.2, we may present the optimal feedback function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^*_N$$\end{document}θN∗ in such a way that96\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \theta ^*(t,\mu ^N)&:=\theta ^*_N\big (t,x,\nabla _xV_N(t,x)\big )\nonumber \\&=\underset{\theta \in \Theta }{\text {arg}\,\text {min\, }}\bigg \{\frac{\lambda }{2}|\theta |^2+\int _{\mathbb {R}}f(t,\theta ,y,\mu ^N)\partial _\mu \mathcal{V}(t,\mu ^N,y)\mu ^N(dy)\bigg \}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}θ∗(t,μN):=θN∗(t,x,∇xVN(t,x))=argmin\,θ∈Θ{λ2|θ|2+∫Rf(t,θ,y,μN)∂μV(t,μN,y)μN(dy)}.Similar to Theorem 2.3, we can show the Lipschitz continuity of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^*(t,\mu ^N)$$\end{document}θ∗(t,μN) in (96), which implies the convergence rate of the optimal feedback function.

Theorem 4.3

Suppose that the assumptions of Lemma 3.9 take place. Then for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} Proof

Up to a duplication, we may assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _1$$\end{document}μ1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _2$$\end{document}μ2 have the same sample size. Denote by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu _1=\frac{1}{N}\sum _{i=1}^N\delta _{x_i},\ \mu _1=\frac{1}{N}\sum _{i=1}^N\delta _{y_i}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μ1=1N∑i=1Nδxi,μ1=1N∑i=1Nδyi.It is easy to see from (96) that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^*(t,x_1,\ldots ,x_N)$$\end{document}θ∗(t,x1,…,xN) on the right hand side remains unchanged after a permutation of the input \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{x_1,\ldots ,x_N\}$$\end{document}{x1,…,xN}. Hence up to a permutation, we may assume that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {W}_1(\mu _1,\mu _2)=\frac{1}{N}\sum _{i=1}^N|x_i-y_i|. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}W1(μ1,μ2)=1N∑i=1N|xi-yi|.Define\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} g(\gamma ):=\theta ^*(t,\gamma {\textbf {x}}_N+(1-\gamma ){\textbf {y}}_N),\ \gamma \in [0,1]. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}g(γ):=θ∗(t,γxN+(1-γ)yN),γ∈[0,1].According to (49) and (78),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\quad |\theta ^*(t,\mu _1)-\theta ^*(t,\mu _2)|=|\theta ^*(t,x_1,\ldots ,x_N)-\theta ^*(t,y_1,\ldots ,y_N)|\nonumber \\&=|g(1)-g(0)|\le \int _0^1|g'(\gamma )|d\gamma \nonumber \\&\le \sum _{l=1}^N|x_l-y_l|\int _0^1\bigg |\frac{\partial }{\partial p_l}\theta ^*+\sum _{k=1}^N\frac{\partial }{\partial q_k}\theta ^*\cdot \partial ^2_{kl}V_N\bigg |(t,\gamma {\textbf {x}}_N+(1-\gamma ){\textbf {y}}_N)d\gamma \nonumber \\&\le \frac{{(\lambda -\lambda _0)^{-1}}C^Q+{(\lambda -\lambda _0)^{-1}}\Vert f_\theta \Vert _\infty \tilde{C}_6}{N}\sum _{l=1}^N|x_l-y_l|\nonumber \\&=({(\lambda -\lambda _0)^{-1}}C^Q+{(\lambda -\lambda _0)^{-1}}\Vert f_\theta \Vert _\infty \tilde{C}_6)\mathcal {W}_1(\mu _1,\mu _2). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|θ∗(t,μ1)-θ∗(t,μ2)|=|θ∗(t,x1,…,xN)-θ∗(t,y1,…,yN)|=|g(1)-g(0)|≤∫01|g′(γ)|dγ≤∑l=1N|xl-yl|∫01|∂∂plθ∗+∑k=1N∂∂qkθ∗·∂kl2VN|(t,γxN+(1-γ)yN)dγ≤(λ-λ0)-1CQ+(λ-λ0)-1‖fθ‖∞C~6N∑l=1N|xl-yl|=((λ-λ0)-1CQ+(λ-λ0)-1‖fθ‖∞C~6)W1(μ1,μ2).□ □

Now we have the convergence rate of feedback function as the sample size grows to infinity.

Corollary 4.4

Suppose that the assumptions of Theorem 4.3 take place and suppose that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu ^N:=\frac{1}{N}\sum _{i=1}^N\delta _{x_i}\rightarrow \mu $$\end{document}μN:=1N∑i=1Nδxi→μ in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {P}_2(\mathbb {R}),\mathcal {W}_{2}),$$\end{document}(P2(R),W2), as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N\rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}N→+∞. Then for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T<\tilde{c}$$\end{document}T<c~ where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{c}$$\end{document}c~ is from Proposition 3.10,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{N\rightarrow +\infty }\theta ^*_N\big (t,x,\nabla _xV_N(t,x)\big )=\theta ^*(t,\mu ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limN→+∞θN∗(t,x,∇xVN(t,x))=θ∗(t,μ),at a rate\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |\theta ^*_N\big (t,x,\nabla _xV_N(t,x)\big )-\theta ^*(t,\mu )|\le \tilde{C}_8\mathcal {W}_1(\mu ^N,\mu ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|θN∗(t,x,∇xVN(t,x))-θ∗(t,μ)|≤C~8W1(μN,μ).

Proof

This is directly from (96) and Theorem 4.3. □ □

Another consequence of Theorem 4.3 is the pathwise convergence at certain rates.

Proof

{P}(\mathbb {R}), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f∗(s,x,μ):=f(s,θ∗(s,μ),x,μ),(s,x,μ)∈[0,T]×R×P(R),then the optimal path \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} Having obtained (99), we may use Theorem 4.3 to further show that for any t∈ [0,T]t∈[0,T],103\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&|\theta ^*_N\big (s,X_N(s),\nabla _xV_N(s,X_N(s))\big )-\theta ^*_M\big (s,X_M(s),\nabla _xV_M(s,X_M(s))\big )|\nonumber \\&\quad =|\theta ^*(s,\mu ^*_N(s))-\theta ^*(s,\mu ^*_M(s))|\le \hat{C}_8\mathcal {W}_1(\mu ^*_N(0),\mu _M(0)). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|θN∗(s,XN(s),∇xVN(s,XN(s)))-θM∗(s,XM(s),∇xVM(s,XM(s)))|=|θ∗(s,μN∗(s))-θ∗(s,μM∗(s))|≤C^8W1(μN∗(0),μM(0)).Passing M to infinity in the above yields the second inequality in (98). □ □

The path \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^*_N\big (s,X_N(s),\nabla _xV_N(s,X_N(s))\big )$$\end{document}θN∗(s,XN(s),∇xVN(s,XN(s))), s∈ [0,T]s∈[0,T] in Proposition 4.5 actually corresponds to the optimal parameters obtained via the neural SDE with N samples. Hence we may interpret Proposition 4.5 in such a way that the aforementioned parameters converge, at a certain rate, as long as the empirical distributions of inputs converge as N tends to infinity.

In addition to the above convergence for short time, we can also obtain the global convergence under assumptions on convexity. We first do some preparation in Lemma 4.6 then present the main results in Theorem 2.5. Denote by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu ^N:=\frac{1}{N}\sum _{i=1}^N\delta _{x_i},\quad \nu ^N:=\frac{1}{N}\sum _{i=1}^N\delta _{y_i}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μN:=1N∑i=1Nδxi,νN:=1N∑i=1Nδyi.

Lemma 4.6

Suppose that the assumptions of Proposition 3.16 take place. Then104\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{1}{N}\sum _{i=1}^N\big |\partial _{\mu }\mathcal{V}(t,\mu ^N,x_i)-\partial _{\mu }\mathcal{V}(t,\nu ^N,y_i)\big |^2\le \frac{\tilde{C}_6}{N}\sum _{i=1}^N|x_i-y_i|^2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}1N∑i=1N|∂μV(t,μN,xi)-∂μV(t,νN,yi)|2≤C~6N∑i=1N|xi-yi|2.

Proof

In view of Theorem 3.18, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(t,x)\in [0,T]\times \mathbb {R}^N$$\end{document}(t,x)∈[0,T]×RN we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \nabla ^2_xV_N(t,x)=\bigg (\nabla ^2_xV_N(t,x)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg )^\top \nabla ^2_xV_N(t,x)^{\frac{1}{2}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∇x2VN(t,x)=(∇x2VN(t,x)12)⊤∇x2VN(t,x)12,for some matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla ^2_xV_N(t,x)^{\frac{1}{2}}\in \mathbb {R}^{N\times N}$$\end{document}∇x2VN(t,x)12∈RN×N such that for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in \mathbb {R}^N$$\end{document}α∈RN with |α |=1|α|=1,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |\nabla ^2_xV_N(t,x)^{\frac{1}{2}}\alpha |\le \sqrt{\frac{\tilde{C}_6}{N}}|\alpha |. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|∇x2VN(t,x)12α|≤C~6N|α|.Therefore for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha ,x,y\in \mathbb {R}^N$$\end{document}α,x,y∈RN,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\langle \alpha ,\nabla _xV_N(t,x)-\nabla _xV_N(t,y)\rangle \nonumber \\&\quad =\int _0^1\bigg \langle \nabla ^2_xV_N(t,y+s(x-y))^{\frac{1}{2}}\alpha ,\nabla ^2_xV_N(t,y+s(x-y))^\frac{1}{2}(x-y)\bigg \rangle ds\nonumber \\&\quad \le \frac{\tilde{C}_6}{N}|\alpha |\cdot |x-y|. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨α,∇xVN(t,x)-∇xVN(t,y)⟩=∫01〈∇x2VN(t,y+s(x-y))12α,∇x2VN(t,y+s(x-y))12(x-y)〉ds≤C~6N|α|·|x-y|.The inequality above implies that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |\nabla _xV_N(t,x)-\nabla _xV_N(t,y)|\le \frac{\tilde{C}_6}{N}|x-y|, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|∇xVN(t,x)-∇xVN(t,y)|≤C~6N|x-y|,which is (104) according to (92). □ □

Lemma 4.6 tells that we can extend the domain of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _\mu \mathcal{V}(t,\nu ,\cdot )$$\end{document}∂μV(t,ν,·) from the set of all empirical measures to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu \in \mathcal {P}_2(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}ν∈P2(R) in some weak sense, which is formalized as follows.

Corollary 4.7

For each t∈ [0,T]t∈[0,T], there exists a Lipschitz continuous mapping \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu =\frac{1}{N}\sum _{i=1}^N\delta _{x_i}$$\end{document}μ=1N∑i=1Nδxi,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym}
