{R})$$\end{document}Φt:P2(R)→P2(R). Moreover, with an abuse of notation, there exists a unique nonnegative function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _t(\mu ,\cdot )\in L^1(\mu ;\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}Φt(μ,·)∈L1(μ;R) such that for any Borel subset \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A\subseteq \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}A⊆R:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Phi _t(\mu )(A)=\int _A\Phi _t(\mu ,y)\mu (dy). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Φt(μ)(A)=∫AΦt(μ,y)μ(dy).Lastly, for any bounded continuous function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi :\ \mathbb {R}\ \rightarrow \ \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}φ:R→R, we have105\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{\epsilon \rightarrow 0+}\epsilon ^{-1}\bigg (\mathcal{V}\big (t,(I+\epsilon \varphi )\sharp \mu \big )-\mathcal{V}(t,\mu )\bigg )&=\int _{\mathbb {R}}\varphi (y)\Phi _t(\mu )(dy)\nonumber \\ &=\int _{\mathbb {R}}\varphi (y)\Phi _t(\mu ,y)\mu (dy). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limϵ→0+ϵ-1(V(t,(I+ϵφ)♯μ)-V(t,μ))=∫Rφ(y)Φt(μ)(dy)=∫Rφ(y)Φt(μ,y)μ(dy).

Proof

Consider the following empirical measures\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu ^N=\frac{1}{N}\sum _{i=1}^N\delta _{x_i},\quad \nu ^N=\frac{1}{N}\sum _{i=1}^N\delta _{y_i}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μN=1N∑i=1Nδxi,νN=1N∑i=1Nδyi.In view of the symmetric property, there is no loss of generality in assuming\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {W}_2(\mu ^N,\nu ^N)=\bigg (\frac{1}{N}\sum _{i=1}^N|x_i-y_i|^2\bigg )^\frac{1}{2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}W2(μN,νN)=(1N∑i=1N|xi-yi|2)12.Then we have from Lemma 4.6 that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\mathcal {W}_2\big (\Phi _t(\mu ^N),\Phi _t(\nu ^N)\big )\le \sqrt{\tilde{C}_6}\mathcal {W}_2(\mu ^N,\nu ^N). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}W2(Φt(μN),Φt(νN))≤C~6W2(μN,νN).Hence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _t$$\end{document}Φt is Lipschitz continuous.

Consider now \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A\cap { \text {supp}}(\mu )=\emptyset $$\end{document}A∩supp(μ)=∅ as well as i.i.d random variables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\xi _i)_{i\ge 1}$$\end{document}(ξi)i≥1 with law μ μ. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\mu }^N=\frac{1}{N}\sum _{i=1}^N\delta _{\xi _i}$$\end{document}μ^N=1N∑i=1Nδξi. It holds that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi _i\notin A,\ i\ge 1$$\end{document}ξi∉A,i≥1 a.s., hence\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {E}[\Phi _t(\hat{\mu }^N)(A)]=\mathbb {E}\bigg [\int _A\partial _\mu \mathcal{V}(t,\hat{\mu }^N,y)\hat{\mu }^N(dy)\bigg ]=0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E[Φt(μ^N)(A)]=E[∫A∂μV(t,μ^N,y)μ^N(dy)]=0.In view of the strong law of large numbers and that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _t$$\end{document}Φt is Lipschitz continuous, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _t(\hat{\mu }^N)(A)\rightarrow \Phi _t(\mu )(A)$$\end{document}Φt(μ^N)(A)→Φt(μ)(A) a.s.. Bounded convergence theorem yields \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _t(\mu )(A)=0.$$\end{document}Φt(μ)(A)=0. Notice that A is arbitrary such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A\cap {\text { supp}}(\mu )=\emptyset $$\end{document}A∩supp(μ)=∅, we have that the Radon–Nikodym derivative \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _t(\mu ,\cdot )\in L^1(\mu ;\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}Φt(μ,·)∈L1(μ;R). Before going into the next convergence result, we would like to discuss Corollary 4.7 from the perspective of PDE. In view of (105), it is natural to define the derivative \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _\mu \mathcal{V}$$\end{document}∂μV in the following way:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _\mu \mathcal{V}(t,\mu ,y):=\Phi (t,\mu ,y),\quad (t,\mu ,y)\in [0,T]\times \mathbb {P}_2(\mathbb {R})\times \mathbb {R}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂μV(t,μ,y):=Φ(t,μ,y),(t,μ,y)∈[0,T]×P2(R)×R.It is easy to see that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _\mu \mathcal{V}(t,\mu ^N,x_1)=NV_N(t,x_1,\ldots ,x_N)$$\end{document}∂μV(t,μN,x1)=NVN(t,x1,…,xN). When σ =0σ=0, in view of the projection property in Theorem 2.3, it is straightforward that (3) is true for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu =\mu ^N$$\end{document}μ=μN. Thanks to Corollary 4.7 and the above notion of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _\mu \mathcal{V}$$\end{document}∂μV, we may now let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu ^N\rightarrow \mu $$\end{document}μN→μ and find (3) true for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu \in \mathcal {P}_2(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}μ∈P2(R).

As a result of the distributional difference estimate in Lemma 4.6, we prove the Lipschitz continuity of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^*(t,\mu ^N)$$\end{document}θ∗(t,μN) for long time T>0T>0, which has been reported in Theorem 2.5.

Proof of Theorem 2.5

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta ^*$$\end{document}θ∗, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\theta }^*$$\end{document}θ^∗ denote the optimal feedback function corresponding to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu ^N$$\end{document}μN and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu ^N$$\end{document}νN. Lemma 5.1

Suppose (107) and (108). Then for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p=(p_1,\ldots ,p_N)\in \mathbb {R}^N$$\end{document}p=(p1,…,pN)∈RN satisfying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_i\ge 0$$\end{document}pi≥0,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} D^2_{xx}\tilde{H}_N(t,x,p)\ge 0,\quad (t,x)\in [0,T]\times \mathbb {R}^N. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Dxx2H~N(t,x,p)≥0,(t,x)∈[0,T]×RN.

Proof

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi =(\xi _1,\ldots ,\xi _N)\in \mathbb {R}^N$$\end{document}ξ=(ξ1,…,ξN)∈RN, direct calculation yields\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\xi ^\top D^2_{xx}\tilde{H}_N(t,x,p)\xi \\&\quad =-\bigg (\sum _{i=1}^N\partial ^2_{\theta \theta }f(t,x_i)p_i+\lambda \bigg )^{-1}\bigg (\sum _{i,j=1}^N\xi We also need the following monotonicity:

Lemma 5.2

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^\varepsilon _N$$\end{document}VNε be the solution to (59) with parameters from (106)–(108), then for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(t,x)\in [0,T]\times \mathbb {R}^N,\ 1\le i\le N$$\end{document}(t,x)∈[0,T]×RN,1≤i≤N, it holds that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _{x_i}V^\varepsilon _N(t,x)\ge 0.$$\end{document}∂xiVNε(t,x)≥0.

Proof

In view of Proposition 3.11, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V^\varepsilon _N$$\end{document}VNε is the classical solution to HJB Eq. (59). We may consider the corresponding stochastic optimal control problem and use the stochastic maximum principle to get the optimal path and the adjoint process. Then for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(t,x)\in [0,T]\times \mathbb {R}^N$$\end{document}(t,x)∈[0,T]×RN we obtain the following:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\{ \begin{aligned} dX^{i,*}_N(s)&=f\big (s,X^{i,*}_N(s),\mu ^*_N(s)\big )ds+\sigma dW(s),\\ dY^{*,i}_N(s)&=-\bigg [\partial _xf\big (s,\theta (s),X^{\theta ,i}_N(s)\big )Y^{*,i}_N(s)\\&\qquad +\frac{1}{N}L'\big (X^{i,*}_N(s)\big )\bigg ]ds+\sum _{j=0}^NZ^{ij}_N(s)dW^j(s),\ s\in [t,T],\\ X^*_N(t)&=x,\quad Y^{*,i}_N(T)=\frac{1}{N}U'\big (X^{*,i}_N(T)\big ),\quad 1\le i\le N. \end{aligned}\right. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}dXNi,∗(s)=f(s,XNi,∗(s),μN∗(s))ds+σdW(s),dYN∗,i(s)=-[∂xf(s,θ(s),XNθ,i(s))YN∗,i(s)+1NL′(XNi,∗(s))]ds+∑j=0NZNij(s)dWj(s),s∈[t,T],XN∗(t)=x,YN∗,i(T)=1NU′(XN∗,i(T)),1≤i≤N.Define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{Y}^{*,i}_N(s)=\exp \bigg \{\int _t^s\partial _xf\big (s,\theta (s),X^{\theta ,i}_N(s)\big )ds\bigg \}Y^{*,i}_N(s)$$\end{document}Y~N∗,i(s)=exp{∫ts∂xf(s,θ(s),XNθ,i(s))ds}YN∗,i(s). Note that L',U'≥ 0L′,U′≥0, then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\quad \partial _{x_i}V^\varepsilon _N(t,x)=\tilde{Y}^{*,i}_N(t)\\&=\mathbb {E}\bigg [\frac{1}{N}U'\big (X^{*,i}_N(T)\big )+\int _t^T\frac{1}{N}\exp \bigg \{\int _t^s\partial _xf\big (s,\theta (s),X^{\theta ,i}_N(s)\big )ds\bigg \}L'\big (X^{i,*}_N(s)\big )ds\bigg ]\ge 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂xiVNε(t,x)=Y~N∗,i(t)=E[1NU′(XN∗,i(T))+∫tT1Nexp{∫ts∂xf(s,θ(s),XNθ,i(s))ds}L′(XNi,∗(s))ds]≥0.□ □

With the above preparation, we can now show the quantitative convergence results on the value functions and training outcomes of the current neural SDE where the coefficient of the regularizer λ >0λ>0 can be arbitrarily small.

Theorem 5.3

The the conditions in Proposition 3.16 are fulfilled with arbitrary \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda ,\ T>0$$\end{document}λ,T>0. As a consequence, the convergence results in Theorems 2.3, 2.5 and Proposition 2.7 are valid.

Proof

Fix arbitrary λ >0λ>0. In view of Lemmas 3.4 and 5.2, we can see that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (x,\nabla _xV^\varepsilon _N(t,x)\big )\in \mathcal {A}_{N,0,\tilde{C}_2}$$\end{document}(x,∇xVNε(t,x))∈AN,0,C~2 for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon >0,\ (t,x)\in [0,T]\times \mathbb {R}^N$$\end{document}ε>0,(t,x)∈[0,T]×RN. Moreover, since L,\ UL,U are Lipischitz, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^L_{11}+C^U_{11}=0$$\end{document}C11L+C11U=0. Note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial ^2_{\theta \theta }f>0$$\end{document}∂θθ2f>0 and is bounded, hence for any constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _0\in (0,\lambda )$$\end{document}λ0∈(0,λ), (46) is true for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(t,\theta ,x,p)\in [0,T]\times \Theta \times \mathcal {A}_{N,\delta ,\tilde{C}_2}$$\end{document}(t,θ,x,p)∈[0,T]×Θ×AN,δ,C~2 with sufficientlyl small δ >0δ>0. Since f has bounded derivatives, (47) holds trivially with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Gamma _1,\Gamma _2)=(\delta ,\tilde{C}_2)$$\end{document}(Γ1,Γ2)=(δ,C~2). Hence

Hypothesis (R) holds with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Gamma _1,\Gamma _2)=(\delta ,\tilde{C}_2)$$\end{document}(Γ1,Γ2)=(δ,C~2). Combining Lemmas 5.1 and 5.2, we obtain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla ^2_{xx}\tilde{H}_N\big (t,x,\nabla _xV^\varepsilon _N(t,x)\big )\ge 0$$\end{document}∇xx2H~N(t,x,∇xVNε(t,x))≥0 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N\ge 1,\ \varepsilon >0$$\end{document}N≥1,ε>0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(t,x)\in [0,T]\times \mathbb {R}^N$$\end{document}(t,x)∈[0,T]×RN. Therefore

Hypothesis (R1) holds with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Gamma _1,\Gamma _2)=(\delta ,\tilde{C}_2)$$\end{document}(Γ1,Γ2)=(δ,C~2) and the proof is complete. □ □
